far  $\{(x_{i,t},a_{i,t},y_{i,t},x_{i,t + 1})\}_{0\leq t < t(k - 1),1\leq i\leq n}$  and use an  $\epsilon$ -greedy method to generate actions in the next block of data. in our experiments, we set  $\epsilon = 0.2$ ,  $k = 4$  and  $t(1) = t(2) = \dots = t(k) = t$ . we fix  $n = 25$  and consider three choices of  $t$ , corresponding to  $t = 120$ , 200 and 280. we consider three choices of  $\mathbb{g}$ , corresponding to  $\mathbb{g} = \delta_{(0.5,0.5)}$ ,  $\delta_{(-0.5, - 0.5)}$  and  $n(0_2,i_2)$ . the true optimal value function is approximated by monte carlo methods, as in off-policy settings. ecps and als of the proposed cis are reported in table 3. it can be seen that ecps are close to the nominal level in almost all cases and als decrease as  $t$  increases.

# 6 application to the ohiot1dm dataset

as commented in the introduction, this dataset contains eight weeks' records of cgm blood glucose levels, insulin doses and self-reported life-event data for each of six patients with type 1 diabetes. to analyze this data, we divide these eight weeks into three hour intervals. the state variable  $x_{i,t}$  is set to be a three-dimensional vector. specifically, its first element  $x_{i,t}^{(1)}$  is the average cgm blood glucose levels during the three hour interval  $[t - 1,t)$ . the second covariate  $x_{i,t}^{(2)}$  is constructed based on the  $i$ -patient's self-reported time and the carbohydrate estimate for the meal. suppose the patient has meals at time  $t_1, t_2, \ldots, t_n \in [t - 1,t)$  with the carbohydrate estimates  $\mathrm{ce}_1, \mathrm{ce}_2, \ldots, \mathrm{ce}_n$ . define

$$
x _ {i, t} ^ {(2)} = \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {n} \mathrm {c e} _ {j} \gamma_ {c} ^ {3 6 (t _ {j} - t + 1)},
$$

where  $\gamma_{c}$  corresponds to the decay rate every five minutes. here, we set  $\gamma_{c} = 0.5$ . the third covariate  $x_{i,t}^{(3)}$  is defined as an average of the basal rate during the three hour interval.

we discretize the action according to the amount of insulin injected in the three hour interval. specifically,  $a_{i,t} = 1$  when the total amount of insulin delivered to the  $i$ -th patient

![](images/ccdddda7c5a0ff415587d3e8c7f82e40471ed777fda855f7aeaab15c93648b31.jpg)  
figure 4: confidence interval of the value difference between an estimated optimal policy and the behavior policy for each of the six patients, with  $\gamma = 0.5$ .

![](images/59923f3f814c8aca59f343090854a5b908b158ecbe0457afba9e9309f72f2a46.jpg)

![](images/bb0e549a881d03f6bb1d66f2350f4a235d0ab96e456f98a59d63c77ac6522736.jpg)  
figure 5: confidence intervals of the value difference between an estimated optimal policy and the behavior policy for each of the six patients, with different  $\gamma$ .

![](images/ced8ee82b123222a8f733acbae631797f89e5d09dd80dd0315b71f15db9fa7a2.jpg)

is greater than one unit. otherwise, we set  $a_{i,t} = 0$ . the immediate reward  $y_{i,t}$  is defined according to the index of glycemic control (igc, rodbard, 2009), which is a non-linear function of the blood glucose levels. specifically, we set

$$
y _ {i, t} = \left\{ \begin{array}{r l r} & {- \frac {1}{3 0} (8 0 - x _ {i, t + 1} ^ {(1)}) ^ {2},} & {x _ {i, t + 1} ^ {(1)} <   8 0;} \\ & {0,} & {8 0 \leq x _ {i, t + 1} ^ {(1)} <   1 4 0;} \\ & {- \frac {1}{3 0} (x _ {i, t + 1} ^ {(1)} - 1 4 0) ^ {1. 3 5},} & {1 4 0 \leq x _ {i, t + 1} ^ {(1)}.} \end{array} \right.
$$

a large igc indicates the patient is in good health status. we set the discount factor  $\gamma = 0.5$ , as in simulations.

for the  $i$ -th patient, we apply the double fqi algorithm to the data

$$
\{(x _ {i, t}, a _ {i, t}, y _ {i, t}, x _ {i, t + 1}) \} _ {0 \leq t <   t},
$$

to estimate a patient-specific optimal policy. then we compute the estimator for the value function  $v(\pi^{opt}; x_{i,0})$  starting from the initial state variable  $x_{i,0}$ . in addition, we extend our methodology in section 5.2 to construct the confidence interval for the value difference  $v(\pi^{opt}; x_{i,0}) - v(b; x_{i,0})$  where  $v(b; x_{i,0})$  corresponds to the value under the behavior policy. see appendix b.2 for details. in figure 4, we plot our proposed ci for the value difference, for each of the six patients, when the initial starting time is either 8:00 am or 2:00 pm in day 1. it can be seen that the estimated value differences are strictly positive in all cases. this implies that the optimal value is strictly larger than the observed discounted cumulative reward. in some cases, the lower bound of our ci is larger than zero. the difference is thus significant. in figure 5, we fix the starting time to 8:00 am and plot the ci of the value difference with different  $\gamma$ . results show a similar qualitative pattern. this suggests applying reinforcement learning algorithms could potentially improve some patients' health status.

# 7 discussion

# 7.1 comparison between drl

we discuss the advantages and limitations among the proposed method and drl when inferring the value under a fixed decision rule. generally speaking, the proposed method results in narrower cis and would be preferred in cases where  $(nt)^{-1/2}$ -consistent estimation of the q-function is feasible. this includes settings where the dimension of the state-vector is not large, as in our real data applications. in contrast, drl would be preferred in ergodic environments with high-dimensional covariates where  $(nt)^{-1/2}$ -consistent estimation of the q-function is infeasible.

specifically, in appendix c.2, we consider settings where both the behavior policy and the target policy are nondynamic. under certain conditions, we prove that the variance of the drl estimator is strictly larger than that of the proposed estimator. this in turn implies that our method yields a narrower ci in general.

in addition, we remark that the ci constructed by drl requires the data to be generated from an ergodic environment. in the cliff walking example, the data are generated by a mixture of the optimal and random policy. since the agent will be sent instantly to the starting point wherever it steps into the cliff or arrives at the destination, the markov chain formed by the state-action pair is no longer ergodic. it can be seen from table 1 where ecp of the ci is well below the nominal level in the cliff walking example. although our procedure also requires the ergodicity assumption (see condition (a3)(ii)), this assumption is not necessary. it can be seen from the proof of theorem 1 that our ci is valid as long as the random matrix  $\widehat{\sigma}_{\pi}$  stabilizes. this is consistent with the findings in table 1 where our ci achieves nominal coverage in all cases.

however, to ensure the proposed ci is valid, we require the bias of our q-estimator to decay at a rate of  $o((nt)^{-1/2})$ . consequently, our estimator converges at a rate of  $(nt)^{-1/2}$ . this rate might not be achievable in high-dimensions. in contrast, drl requires a weaker condition. the ci based on drl is valid when both the q-estimator and the estimated marginalized density ratio converge at a rate of  $o((nt)^{-1/4})$ .

another potential limitation of our method is that in cases where  $\widehat{\sigma}_{\pi}$  is close to a singular matrix, the resulting q-estimator might suffer from over-fitting, leading to an unbounded outcome. in practice, we could add a ridge penalty to reduce over-fitting. we discuss in detail in appendix d.4.

# 7.2 number of basis functions

we outline a procedure to choose the number of basis function  $l$  in this section. the idea is to simulate the model dynamics and select  $l$  such that the resulting confidence interval achieves nominal coverage under the simulated model. specifically, given the observed data  $\{(x_{i,t}, a_{i,t}, y_{i,t}, x_{i,t+1})\}_{0 \leq t < t_i, 1 \leq i \leq n}$ , we propose to learn the conditional density function of  $(y_{i,t}, x_{i,t+1})$  given  $(a_{i,t}, x_{i,t})$ . following janner et al. (2019), we recommend to use a gaussian distribution to model the conditional density function in practice,

$$
(y _ {i, t}, x _ {i, t + 1}) | (a _ {i, t}, x _ {i, t}) = (a, x) \sim n (\mu (a, x), \sigma (a, x)). \tag {7.22}
$$

the conditional mean  $\mu$  can be estimated via nonparametric regression (e.g., random forest). let  $\widehat{\mu}$  denote the corresponding estimator. given the set of estimated residuals

$\{(y_{i,t},x_{i,t + 1})^\top -\widehat{\mu} (a_{i,t},x_{i,t})\}_{i,t}$  the conditional covariance function  $\sigma$  can be estimated via regression as well. we remark that in addition to the gaussian function, other density functions could be used to model the system dynamics as well.

the behavior policy  $b$  can be similarly estimated via regression. given an estimated behavior policy  $\widehat{b}$  and  $\widehat{\mu}, \widehat{\sigma}$ , we generate simulated trajectories to investigate the performance of the proposed ci with different choices of  $l$ .

finally, we choose  $l$  such that the resulting ci is the shortest among all cis whose coverage probabilities are above certain level (e.g., 93%) under the simulated environment.

in appendix d.1, we investigate the finite sample performance of such a method and find that it performs reasonably well. we remark that alternative to the aforementioned method, cross-validation could be applied to select  $l$ .

# 7.3 sensitivity to the ordering of trajectories

the proposed sequential value evaluation procedure in section 3.2 divides the data into blocks defined both by trajectories and by time. while there is a natural order in time, there does not appear to be a natural order in the trajectories. in appendix d.2.3, we conduct additional simulation studies to investigate the sensitivity of our ci to the ordering of trajectories under scenario (b). results suggest that our ci is not overly sensitive under our simulation setting.

as suggested by one of the referees, we may aggregate cis over multiple orderings in cases where the results depend strongly on the ordering of the trajectories. dezeure et al. (2015) derived a ci for the regression coefficients in high-dimensional models by aggregating results over multiple sample splits using a quantile function. we can adopt their method to aggregate our cis over multiple orderings. alternatively, one may average the value estimates over sufficiently many orderings and apply similar methods developed in wang et al. (2020); shi et al. (2020a) to derive the ci. however, these algorithms are much more time-consuming.

# 7.4 more on value-based method

in section 3.1.1, we discuss a potential drawback of using nonparametric methods to directly model the value function. we remark that a kernel-type importance sampling estimator

for the  $v(\pi ;x)$  will not suffer from this issue, since it does not directly model the value function, but uses an inverse propensity-score weighted method instead. both ipw and regression type estimators have their own merits. in general, ipwes might suffer from a large variance whereas regression-based estimators might suffer from a large bias. there exist methods that combine both for more robust off-policy evaluation (see e.g., kallus and uehara, 2019; uehara et al., 2020; tang et al., 2020; shi et al., 2021). however, as commented in section 7.1, they might yield larger cis compared to our method.

# 7.5 rate of convergence of q-learning type algorithms

through authors' communication, we found a recent independent work by hu et al. (2021) that derived a nonasymptotic error bound on the value of the estimated optimal policy computed by q-learning type algorithms under the margin condition. their results are consistent with our theoretical findings in theorems 3 and 4 that show the value of the estimated optimal policy converges to the optimal value at a faster rate than the estimated q-function.

# a some technical conditions

# a.1 more on conditions a3

define  $\mathcal{p}_x^t$  as the  $t$ -step transition kernel, i.e.,  $\mathcal{p}_x^t (\mathcal{b}|x) = \operatorname*{pr}(x_{0,t}\in \mathcal{b}|x_{0,0} = x)$ . geometric ergodicity implies that there exists some function  $m(\cdot)$  on  $\mathbb{x}$  and some constant  $\rho < 1$  such that  $\int_{x\in \mathbb{x}}m(x)\mu (x)dx < +\infty$  and

$$
\| \mathcal {p} _ {x} ^ {t} (\cdot | x) - \mu (\cdot) \| _ {t v} \leq m (x) \rho^ {t}, \quad \forall t \geq 0,
$$

where  $\| \cdot \|_{tv}$  denotes the total variation norm.

# a.2 conditions a3*

we present the technical condition (a3*) below. we assume the estimated policy satisfies  $\widehat{\pi}_{\mathcal{i}} \in \pi$  with probability 1, for any  $\mathcal{i}$ . for example, if q-learning type algorithms are used and we approximate the optimal q-function based on a linear model  $\phi^{\top}(x,a)\beta$  with some basis function  $\phi(x,a) \in \mathbb{r}^{\mathbb{m}}$ . then for any  $\beta$ , we can define a policy  $\pi(\beta)$  as follows:

$$
\pi_ {\beta} (a | x) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} 1, & \mathrm {i f} a = \operatorname {s a r g m a x} _ {a ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {a}} \phi^ {\top} (x, a ^ {\prime}) \beta , \\ 0, & \mathrm {o t h e r w i s e .} \end{array} \right.
$$

then we have  $\pi = \{\pi_{\beta}:\beta \in \mathbb{r}^{\mathbb{m}}\}$

(a3\*.) assume (i) and (ii) hold if  $t \to \infty$  and (i) holds if  $t$  is bounded.

(i)  $\inf_{\pi \in \pi} \lambda_{\min} \left[ \sum_{t=0}^{t-1} \mathbb{e} \left\{ \pmb{\xi}_{0,t} \pmb{\xi}_{0,t}^{\top} - \gamma^{2} \pmb{u}_{\pi}(x_{0,t}, a_{0,t}) \pmb{u}_{\pi}^{\top}(x_{0,t}, a_{0,t}) \right\} \right] \geq \bar{c} t$  for some constant  $c > 0$ .  
(ii) the markov chain  $\{x_{0,t}\}_{t\geq 0}$  is geometrically ergodic.

we remark that condition a3\* (ii) is the same as a3(ii).

# a.3 more on the margin condition

to better understand condition a5, we consider a simple scenario where  $\mathcal{a} = \{0,1\}$ . define  $\tau(x) = q^{opt}(x,1) - q^{opt}(x,0)$ . it follows that

$$
\max _ {a} q ^ {o p t} (x, a) - \max _ {a ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {a} - \arg \max _ {a} q ^ {o p t} (x, a)} q ^ {o p t} (x, a ^ {\prime}) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} | \tau (x) |, & \mathrm {i f} \tau (x) \neq 0, \\ + \infty , & \mathrm {o t h e r w i s e .} \end{array} \right.
$$

as a result, (3.18) and (3.19) are equivalent to the followings:

$$
\lambda \left\{x \in \mathbb {x}: 0 <   | \tau (x) | \leq \varepsilon \right\} = o \left(\varepsilon^ {\alpha}\right), \tag {a.23}
$$

$$
\mathbb {g} \left\{x \in \mathbb {x}: 0 <   | \tau (x) | \leq \varepsilon \right\} = o \left(\varepsilon^ {\alpha}\right). \tag {a.24}
$$

apparently, these two conditions hold when  $\inf_{x\in \mathbb{x}}|\tau (x)| > 0$ . they are satisfied in many other cases. for example, let  $d = 1$ . consider

$$
\tau (x) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} x ^ {1 / \alpha}, & \mathrm {i f} x > 0, \\ 0, & \mathrm {o t h e r w i s e}, \end{array} \right.
$$

for some  $\alpha > 0$ . then, with some calculations, we can show

$$
\lambda \left\{x \in \mathbb {x}: 0 <   | \tau (x) | \leq \varepsilon \right\} \leq \lambda \left\{x: 0 <   x <   \varepsilon^ {\alpha} \right\} = \varepsilon^ {\alpha}.
$$

this verifies (a.23). when  $\mathbb{g}$  has a bounded density function on  $\mathbb{x}$ , (a.24) is reduced to (a.23). if  $\mathbb{g}(\cdot)$  equals the dirac measure  $\delta_x(\cdot)$ , then (a.24) automatically holds for any  $\alpha > 0$ .

# b additional details regarding the method

# b.1 more on the ci in (3.17)

we begin by providing more details on the estimators  $\widehat{v}_{\mathcal{i}_{k + 1}}(\widehat{\pi}_{\bar{\mathcal{i}}_k};\mathbb{g})$  and its standard error  $\widehat{\sigma}_{\mathcal{i}_{k + 1}}(\widehat{\pi}_{\bar{\mathcal{i}}_k};\mathbb{g})$ . in general, for a given  $\mathcal{i} \subseteq \mathcal{i}_0$  and any policy  $\pi$ , we define  $\widehat{v}_{\mathcal{i}}(\pi ;\mathbb{g})$  and

$\widehat{\sigma}_{\mathcal{i}}(\pi ;\mathbb{g})$  as

$$
\widehat {v} _ {\mathcal {i}} (\pi ; \mathbb {g}) = \left\{\int_ {x \in \mathbb {x}} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\pi} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\} ^ {\top} \widehat {\beta} _ {\mathcal {i}, \pi},
$$

$$
\widehat {\sigma} _ {\mathcal {i}} ^ {2} (\pi ; \mathbb {g}) = \left\{\int_ {x \in \mathbb {x}} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\pi} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\} ^ {\top} \widehat {\boldsymbol {\sigma}} _ {\mathcal {i}, \pi} ^ {- 1} \widehat {\boldsymbol {\omega}} _ {\mathcal {i}, \pi} (\widehat {\boldsymbol {\sigma}} _ {\mathcal {i}, \pi} ^ {\top}) ^ {- 1} \left\{\int_ {x \in \mathbb {x}} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\pi} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\},
$$

where

$$
\widehat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {\mathcal {i}, \pi} = (\widehat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {\mathcal {i}, \pi , 1} ^ {\top}, \dots , \widehat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {\mathcal {i}, \pi , m} ^ {\top}) ^ {\top} = \frac {1}{| \mathcal {i} |} \sum_ {(i, t) \in \mathcal {i}} \widehat {\boldsymbol {\sigma}} _ {\mathcal {i}, \pi} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {i, t} y _ {i, t}, \quad \widehat {\boldsymbol {\sigma}} _ {\mathcal {i}, \pi} = \frac {1}{| \mathcal {i} |} \sum_ {(i, t) \in \mathcal {i}} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {i, t} (\boldsymbol {\xi} _ {i, t} - \gamma \boldsymbol {u} _ {\pi , i, t + 1}) ^ {\top},
$$

$$
\widehat {\boldsymbol {\omega}} _ {\mathcal {i}, \pi} = \frac {1}{| \mathcal {i} |} \sum_ {(i, t) \in \mathcal {i}} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {i, t} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {i, t} ^ {\top} \{y _ {i, t} + \gamma \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {a}} \phi_ {l} ^ {\top} (x _ {i, t + 1}) \widehat {\beta} _ {\mathcal {i}, \pi , a} \pi (a | x _ {i, t + 1}) - \phi_ {l} ^ {\top} (x _ {i, t}) \widehat {\beta} _ {\pi , a _ {i, t}} \} ^ {2},
$$

and  $|\mathcal{i}|$  stands for the number of elements in  $\mathcal{i}$ .

# b.2 value difference between the target and behavior policy

in this section, we outline a method to evaluate the value difference function between the target and behavior policy. we first consider the scenario where the target policy is a fixed policy. we next consider the scenario where the target policy is an estimated optimal policy. to simplify the presentation, we assume  $t_{1} = \dots = t_{n} = t$ . the proposed method can be similarly extended to on-policy settings.

# b.2.1 inference of the value difference under a fixed policy

consider a data-independent policy  $\pi$ . we aim to evaluate the value difference function  $\mathrm{vd}(\pi; x) = v(\pi; x) - v(b; x)$  where  $b$  is the unknown behavior policy. we first apply our method in section 3.1.2 to compute an estimator value function  $\widehat{v}(\pi; x)$  for  $v(\pi; x)$ .

to estimate  $v(b; x)$ , we observe that the q-function  $q(b; x, a)$  satisfies the bellman equation,  $\mathbb{e}[\{y_{i,t} + \gamma q(b; x_{i,t+1}, a_{i,t+1}) - q(b; x_{i,t}, a_{i,t})\}|x_{i,t}, a_{i,t}] = 0$ . we approximate  $q(b; x, a)$  based on linear sieves  $\phi_l^\top(x)\beta_{b,a}^*$ . similar to section 3.1.2,  $\beta_b = (\beta_{b,1}^{*\top}, \dots, \beta_{b,m}^{*\top})^\top$  can be estimated by

$$
\widehat {\pmb {\beta}} _ {b} = (\widehat {\beta} _ {b, 1} ^ {\top}, \dots , \widehat {\beta} _ {b, m} ^ {\top}) ^ {\top} = \left\{\underbrace {\frac {1}{n t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \pmb {\xi} _ {i , t} (\pmb {\xi} _ {i , t} - \gamma \pmb {\xi} _ {i , t + 1}) ^ {\top}} _ {\widehat {\pmb {\sigma}} _ {b}} \right\} ^ {- 1} \left(\frac {1}{n t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \pmb {\xi} _ {i, t} y _ {i, t}\right).
$$

the resulting estimates for  $q(b; x, a)$  can be derived as  $\phi_l^\top(x) \widehat{\beta}_{b,a}$ . the corresponding estimator for  $v(b, x)$  is given by  $\widehat{v}(b, x) = \sum_{a} \widehat{b}(a|x) \phi_l^\top(x) \widehat{\beta}_{b,a}$  where  $\widehat{b}(a|x)$  denotes the sieve estimator  $\phi_l^\top(x) \widehat{\alpha}_a$  for  $b(a|x)$  where

$$
\widehat {\alpha} _ {a} = \left\{\underbrace {\frac {1}{n t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \psi_ {l} (x _ {i , t}) \psi_ {l} ^ {\top} (x _ {i , t})} _ {\widehat {\psi}} \right\} ^ {- 1} \left\{\frac {1}{n t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \psi_ {l} (x _ {i, t}) \mathbb {i} (a _ {i, t} = a) \right\}.
$$

this yields the estimator for the value difference  $\widehat{\mathrm{vd}} (\pi ;x) = \widehat{v} (\pi ;x) - \widehat{v} (b;x)$ .

we next derive a confidence interval for  $\mathrm{vd}(\pi ;x)$  based on  $\widehat{\mathrm{vd}} (\pi ;x)$ . similar to the proof of theorem 1, we can show  $\sqrt{nt}\{\widehat{\mathrm{vd}} (\pi ;x) - \mathrm{vd}(\pi ;x)\}$  is equivalent to

$$
\begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\pi} ^ {\top} (x) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\pi} ^ {- 1} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {n t}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {i, t} \varepsilon_ {i, t}\right) - \boldsymbol {u} _ {b} ^ {\top} (x) \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {b} ^ {- 1} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {n t}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {i, t} \varepsilon_ {i, t} ^ {*}\right) \\ - \sum_ {a} q (b; a, x) \psi_ {l} ^ {\top} (x) \psi^ {- 1} \left[ \frac {1}{\sqrt {n t}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \psi_ {l} \left(x _ {i, t}\right) \left\{\mathbb {i} \left(a _ {i, t} = a\right) - b (a | x _ {i, t}) \right\} \right], \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\varepsilon_{i,t}^{*}$  denotes the temporal difference error  $y_{i,t} + \gamma q(b;x_{i,t + 1},a_{i,t + 1}) - q(b;x_{i,t},a_{i,t})$  and  $\psi$  denotes the population limit of  $\widehat{\psi}$ . note that the rhs can be rewritten as  $(nt)^{-1 / 2}\sum_{i = 1}^{n}\sum_{t = 0}^{t - 1}\psi_{i,t}$  that corresponds to a sum of martingale difference. its variance can be consistently estimated by  $\widehat{\sigma}^{*2}(\pi ;x) = (nt)^{-1}\sum_{i = 1}^{n}\sum_{t = 0}^{t - 1}\widehat{\psi}_{i,t}^2$  where  $\widehat{\psi}_{i,t}$  denotes some consistent estimator for  $\psi_{i,t}$  based on  $\widehat{q} (\pi ;\cdot ,\cdot)$ ,  $\widehat{q} (b;\cdot ,\cdot)$  and  $\widehat{b}$ . the confidence interval for  $\mathrm{vd}(\pi ;x)$  is given by

$$
[ \widehat {\mathrm {v d}} (\pi ; x) - z _ {\alpha / 2} (n t) ^ {- 1 / 2} \widehat {\sigma} ^ {*} (\pi ; x), \widehat {\mathrm {v d}} (\pi ; x) + z _ {\alpha / 2} (n t) ^ {- 1 / 2} \widehat {\sigma} ^ {*} (\pi ; x) ].
$$

# b.2.2 inference of the value difference under an estimated optimal policy

we begin by dividing the data into  $k$  non-overlapping subsets  $\cup_{k=1}^{k}\mathcal{i}_k$ . similar to section 3.2.2, we construct the value difference estimator by

$$
\widetilde {\mathrm {v d}} (x) = \left\{\sum_ {k = 1} ^ {k - 1} \frac {1}{\widehat {\sigma} _ {\mathcal {i} _ {k + 1}} ^ {*} (\widehat {\pi} _ {\bar {\mathcal {i}} _ {k}} ; x)} \right\} ^ {- 1} \left\{\sum_ {k = 1} ^ {k - 1} \frac {\widehat {\mathrm {v d}} _ {\mathcal {i} _ {k + 1}} (\widehat {\pi} _ {\bar {\mathcal {i}} _ {k}} ; x)}{\widehat {\sigma} _ {\mathcal {i} _ {k + 1}} ^ {*} (\widehat {\pi} _ {\bar {\mathcal {i}} _ {k}} ; x)} \right\},
$$

where  $\widehat{\mathrm{vd}}_{\mathcal{i}_k}$  and  $\widehat{\sigma}_{\mathcal{i}_k}^*$  denote the versions of vd and  $\widehat{\sigma}^*$  based on samples in  $\mathcal{i}_k$  only. the corresponding confidence interval is given by

$$
[ \widetilde {\mathrm {v d}} (x) - z _ {\alpha / 2} \{n t (k - 1) / k \} ^ {- 1 / 2} \widetilde {\sigma} ^ {*} (x), \widetilde {\mathrm {v d}} (x) + z _ {\alpha / 2} \{n t (k - 1) / k \} ^ {- 1 / 2} \widetilde {\sigma} ^ {*} (x) ],
$$

where  $\widetilde{\sigma}^{*}(x) = (k - 1)\{\sum_{k = 1}^{k - 1}\widehat{\sigma}_{k}^{* - 1}(\widehat{\pi}_{\bar{\mathcal{i}}_{k}};x)\}^{-1}$ .

finally, we remark that such a confidence interval might not be valid in the extreme case where the behavior policy is equal to a deterministic optimal policy. to elaborate, notice that when the behavior policy is deterministic, the second line of (b.25) equals zero. in addition, when  $\pi = b = \pi^{opt}$  for some optimal policy  $\pi^{opt}$ , the first line equals zero as well. in that case,  $\widehat{\mathrm{vd}} (\pi ;x)$  would have a degenerate distribution. suppose the estimated optimal policy is consistent for  $\pi^{opt}$ . then  $\widetilde{\mathrm{vd}} (x)$  might not have a tractable limiting distribution, leading to an invalid confidence interval.

to address this concern, we could redefine the inverse weights  $\widehat{\sigma}_{\mathcal{i}_{k + 1}}^* (\widehat{\pi}_{\overline{\mathcal{i}}_k};x)$  by  $\widehat{\sigma}_{\mathcal{i}_{k + 1}}^* (\widehat{\pi}_{\overline{\mathcal{i}}_k};x,\delta) = \max \{\widehat{\sigma}_{\mathcal{i}_{k + 1}}^* (\widehat{\pi}_{\overline{\mathcal{i}}_k};x),\delta \}$  for some  $\delta >0$  , as in luedtke and van der laan (2017). this guarantees that these inverse weights are strictly greater than zero. a similar approach is employed by shi et al. (2020b) for testing the overall qualitative treatment effects in single-stage decision making. in addition, one could allow  $\delta$  to depend on  $n$  and  $t$  . the resulting confidence interval would be valid as long as  $\delta \gg (nt)^{-1 / 6}$  (see e.g., theorem 3.1 of shi et al., 2020b). however, a potential limitation is that it would yield a conservative confidence interval when the truncation is active, as discussed in luedtke and van der laan (2017).

# b.3 double fitted  $q$ -iteration

in this section, we introduce our algorithm for computing the estimated optimal policy in our numerical studies. the proposed algorithm is based on fqi that recursively updates the estimated optimal q-function by some supervised learning method (see example 2 in section 3.2.3). in fqi, at each iteration, a maximization over estimated q-function is used as an estimate of the maximum of the true q-function. this can lead to a significant positive bias (sutton and barto, 2018). hasselt (2010) proposed a double q-learning method to reduce the maximization bias. here, we apply similar ideas to fqi to compute the estimated optimal policy. we use a pseudocode to summarize our algorithm below.

in algorithm 1, we can apply any non-parametric models  $q(\cdot, ;\theta)$  indexed by  $\theta$  to model the optimal q-function. in our implementation, we set  $q(\cdot, \cdot; \cdot)$  to be a linear combination of tensor product b-spline basis functions.

algorithm 1: double fitted q-iteration algorithm  
input:  $\{(x_{i,t},a_{i,t},y_{i,t},x_{i,t + 1})\}_{0\leq t <   t_i,1\leq i\leq n}$  , initialize parameters  $\widehat{\theta}_a,\widehat{\theta}_b$    
1 while not convergence do   
2 for  $0\leq t <   t_i,1\leq i\leq n$  , construct the target value:  $\begin{array}{r}\widehat{q}_{i,t}^{a} = y_{i,t} + \gamma q(x_{i,t + 1},\arg \max_{a^{\prime}}q(x_{i,t + 1},a^{\prime};\widehat{\theta}_{a}),\widehat{\theta}_{b})\\ \widehat{q}_{i,t}^{b} = y_{i,t} + \gamma q(x_{i,t + 1},\arg \max_{a^{\prime}}q(x_{i,t + 1},a^{\prime};\widehat{\theta}_{b}),\widehat{\theta}_{a}) \end{array}$    
3 update  $\widehat{\theta}_a:\widehat{\theta}_a\gets \arg \min_{\theta_a}\sum_i\sum_t\| q(x_{i,t},a_{i,t};\theta_a) - \widehat{q}_{i,t}^a\| ^2$    
4 update  $\widehat{\theta}_b:\widehat{\theta}_b\gets \arg \min_{\theta_b}\sum_i\sum_t\| q(x_{i,t},a_{i,t};\theta_b) - \widehat{q}_{i,t}^b\| ^2$

# c additional technical details

# c.1 additional details regarding condition a3

when  $\nu_0 = \mu$ , the density function of  $x_{0,1}$  equals  $\mu$  as well. by jensen's inequality, we have for any  $\pmb{v} \in \mathbb{r}^{ml}$  that

$$
\left\{\boldsymbol {v} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\pi} (x, a) \right\} ^ {2} \leq \mathbb {e} [ \left\{\boldsymbol {v} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\pi} (x _ {0, 1}) \right\} ^ {2} | x _ {0, 0} = x, a _ {0, 0} = a ],
$$

and hence

$$
\int_ {x \in \mathbb {x}} \left\{\boldsymbol {v} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\pi} (x, a) \right\} ^ {2} b (a | x) \mu (x) d x \leq \int_ {x \in \mathbb {x}} \left\{\boldsymbol {v} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\pi} (x) \right\} ^ {2} \mu (x) d x.
$$

the matrix

$$
\int_ {x \in \mathbb {x}} \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {a}} \{\pmb {u} _ {\pi} (x) \pmb {u} _ {\pi} ^ {\top} (x) - \pmb {u} _ {\pi} (x, a) \pmb {u} _ {\pi} ^ {\top} (x, a) \} b (a | x) \mu (x) d x
$$

is positive semidefinite. it follows that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \lambda_ {\min } \left[ \int_ {x \in \mathbb {x}} \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {a}} \left\{\boldsymbol {\xi} (x, a) \boldsymbol {\xi} ^ {\top} (x, a) - \gamma^ {2} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\pi} (x, a) \boldsymbol {u} _ {\pi} ^ {\top} (x, a) \right\} b (a | x) \mu (x) d x \right] \\ \geq \lambda_ {\min} \left[ \int_ {x \in \mathbb {x}} \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {a}} \{\pmb {\xi} (x, a) \pmb {\xi} ^ {\top} (x, a) - \gamma^ {2} \pmb {u} _ {\pi} (x) \pmb {u} _ {\pi} ^ {\top} (x) \} b (a | x) \mu (x) d x \right]. \\ \end{array}
$$

when  $\pi$  is a deterministic policy,  $\sum_{a\in \mathcal{a}}\pmb {\xi}(x,a)\pmb{\xi}^{\top}(x,a)b(a|x) - \gamma^{2}\pmb{u}_{\pi}(x)\pmb{u}_{\pi}^{\top}(x)$  is a block diagonal matrix. to show a3(i) holds, it suffices to show

$$
\lambda_ {\min} [ \phi_ {l} (x) \phi_ {l} ^ {\top} (x) \{b (a | x) - \gamma^ {2} \pi (a | x) \} \mu (x) d x ] > 0, \quad \forall a \in \mathcal {a}.
$$

suppose  $b$  is the  $\epsilon$ -greedy policy with respect to  $\pi$ , i.e,  $b(a|x) = \epsilon m^{-1} + (1 - \epsilon)\pi(a|x)$ , for any  $a \in \{1,\dots,m\}$  and  $\epsilon$  satisfies  $\epsilon \leq 1 - \gamma^2$ , we have

$$
\lambda_ {\min} \left[ \int_ {x \in \mathbb {x}} \phi_ {l} (x) \phi_ {l} ^ {\top} (x) \{b (a | x) - \gamma^ {2} \pi (a | x) \} \mu (x) d x \right] \geq \frac {1}{m} \lambda_ {\min} \left\{\int_ {x \in \mathbb {x}} \phi_ {l} (x) \phi_ {l} ^ {\top} (x) \mu (x) d x \right\}.
$$

suppose a2 holds. it suffices to require

$$
\lambda_ {\min } \left\{\int_ {x \in \mathbb {x}} \phi_ {l} (x) \phi_ {l} ^ {\top} (x) d x \right\} > 0. \tag {c.26}
$$

the condition in (c.26) is automatically satisfied (see, e.g., burman and chen, 1989; chen and christensen, 2015).

# c.2 additional details on the variance comparison

we consider a randomized study where  $b(a|x)$  is a constant function of  $x$ . in addition, we assume the target policy is nondynamic, i.e.,  $\pi(a^* | x) = 1$  for some  $1 \leq a^* \leq m$  and any  $x$ . we impose the following conditions.

(c1) the process  $\{x_{0,t}\}_{t\geq 0}$  is stationary.  
(c2) the temporal difference error  $\varepsilon_{0,t}$  is independent of  $(x_{0,t},a_{0,t})$  
(c3)  $\mathbb{e}\{\phi_l(x_{0,t + 1})\phi_l^\top (x_{0,t + 1})|a_{0,t} = a^*\} = \mathbb{e}\phi_l(x_{0,t + 1})\phi_l^\top (x_{0,t + 1}).$

we make some remarks. first, condition (c1) is imposed to simplify the presentation. the same results hold as long as  $\{x_{0,t}\}_{t}$  will converge to its stationary distribution. second, the variances of our estimator and drl are very difficult to analyse in general. conditions (c2)-(c3) are imposed to simplify the calculation. even when these conditions are violated, we expect the variance of the proposed estimator will be smaller in general, as reflected in our numerical study.

theorem 5 assume (c1)-(c3) hold. then the asymptotic variance of the drl estimator is at least  $h^{-1}(\phi_l)\{1 + \text{var}(w(\pi, x_{0,t}))\}$  times larger than the proposed estimator where  $h(\phi_l) = \int_x \phi_l(x) \mathbb{g}(dx) \{\mathbb{e} \phi_l(x_{0,t}) \phi_l^\top(x_{0,t})\}^{-1} \int_x \phi_l(x) \mathbb{g}(dx)$  and  $w$  is the marginalized density ratio (kallus and uehara, 2019).

we next investigate the value of the factor  $h(\phi_l)$ . we set  $\mathbb{g}$  and the stationary distribution of  $x_{0,t}$ , i.e.,  $\mu$  to a uniform distribution on [0, 1]. we consider a polynomial basis

![](images/fb311a80a84767a1bf338664e6136a8915a78ee843af942e53dc5de9feb17651.jpg)  
figure 6:  $h(\phi_{0,l})$  with different choices of  $\phi_{0,l}$ .

function and a b-spline basis function. figure 6 depicts the value of this factor with different choices of  $l$ . we also tried several other combinations of  $\mathbb{g}$  and  $\mu$ , and find this factor is in general smaller than or very close to 1. since  $\operatorname{var}(w(\pi, x_{0,t})) > 0$ , theorem 5 implies that the proposed estimator achieves smaller variance.

we next sketch a few lines to prove theorem 5. based on theorem 1, the asymptotic variance of our estimator is given by

$$
(n t) ^ {- 1} \left\{\int_ {x \in \mathbb {x}} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\pi} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\pi} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\omega} \left(\boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\pi} ^ {- 1}\right) ^ {\top} \left\{\int_ {x \in \mathbb {x}} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\pi} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\}, \tag {c.27}
$$

where  $\omega$  is defined in step 2 of the proof of theorem 1. under (c1) and (c2), we have  $\pmb{\omega} = \mathbb{e}\pmb{\xi}_{0,t}\pmb{\xi}_{0,t}^{\top}\varepsilon_{0,t}^{2} = \sigma_{*}^{2}\mathbb{e}\pmb{\xi}_{0,t}\pmb{\xi}_{0,t}^{\top}$  where  $\sigma_{*}^{2}$  is the variance of  $\varepsilon_{0,t}$ .

using similar arguments in the proof of theorem 16 of kallus and uehara (2019), we can show that the asymptotic variance of the drl estimator equals

$$
\frac {1}{n t (1 - \gamma) ^ {2}} \mathbb {e} w ^ {2} (x _ {0, 0}) \frac {\mathbb {i} (a _ {0 , 0} = a ^ {*})}{b ^ {2} (a _ {0 , 0} | x _ {0 , 0})} \varepsilon_ {0, 0} ^ {2}.
$$

under the given conditions, the above variance is equal to  $\{nt(1 - \gamma)^2 p_{a^*}\}^{-1}\sigma_*^2\{1 + \mathrm{var}(\omega(x_{0,0}))\}$  where  $p_{a^*} = \operatorname*{pr}(a_{0,t} = a^*)$ . consequently, it suffices to show

$$
\left\{\int_ {x \in \mathbb {x}} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\pi} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\pi} ^ {- 1} \mathbb {e} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {0, t} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {0, t} ^ {\top} (\boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\pi} ^ {- 1}) ^ {\top} \left\{\int_ {x \in \mathbb {x}} \boldsymbol {u} _ {\pi} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\} \leq \frac {h (\phi_ {0 , l})}{(1 - \gamma) ^ {2} p _ {a ^ {*}}}. \quad \mathrm {(c . 2 8)}
$$

since  $\pi$  is a nondynamic policy,  $\int_{x\in \mathbb{x}}u_{\pi}(x)\mathbb{g}(dx)$  is a sparse vector that takes the following form:

$$
\big \{\underbrace {0 , \cdots , 0} _ {(a ^ {*} - 1) l \text {m a n y} 0 s}, \int_ {\mathbb {x}} \phi_ {l} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x), \underbrace {0 , \cdots , 0} _ {(m - a ^ {*}) l \text {m a n y} 0 s} \big \}.
$$

by the definition of  $\pmb{\sigma}_{\pi}$  and  $\xi_{0,t}$ , the left-hand-side of (c.28) is equal to

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\{\int_ {\mathbb {x}} \phi_ {l} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\} ^ {\top} [ \mathbb {e} \phi_ {l} (x _ {0, t}) \mathbb {i} (a _ {0, t} = a ^ {*}) \left\{\phi_ {l} (x _ {0, t}) - \gamma \phi_ {l} (x _ {0, t + 1}) \right\} ^ {\top} ] ^ {- 1 ^ {\top}} \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {a ^ {*}} \\ \times \left[ \mathbb {e} \phi_ {l} \left(x _ {0, t}\right) \mathbb {i} \left(a _ {0, t} = a ^ {*}\right) \left\{\phi_ {l} \left(x _ {0, t}\right) - \gamma \phi_ {l} \left(x _ {0, t + 1}\right) \right\} ^ {\top} \right] ^ {- 1} \left\{\int_ {\mathbb {x}} \phi_ {l} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\omega_{a^*} = \mathbb{e}\phi_l(x_{0,t})\mathbb{i}(a_{0,t} = a^*)\phi_l^\top (x_{0,t})$ . using similar arguments in c.1, we can show

$$
\begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \omega_ {a ^ {*}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \left[ \mathbb {e} \phi_ {l} \left(x _ {0, t}\right) \mathbb {i} \left(a _ {0, t} = a ^ {*}\right) \left\{\phi_ {l} \left(x _ {0, t}\right) - \gamma \phi_ {l} \left(x _ {0, t + 1}\right) \right\} \right] \omega_ {a ^ {*}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \boldsymbol {a} \\ = \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \omega_ {a ^ {*}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbb {e} \phi_ {l} (x _ {0, t}) \mathbb {i} (a _ {0, t} = a ^ {*}) \phi_ {l} ^ {\top} (x _ {0, t}) \omega_ {a ^ {*}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \boldsymbol {a} \\ - \gamma \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {a ^ {*}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbb {e} \phi_ {l} (x _ {0, t}) \mathbb {i} (a _ {0, t} = a ^ {*}) \phi_ {l} ^ {\top} (x _ {0, t + 1}) \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {a ^ {*}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \boldsymbol {a} \\ \geq \| \boldsymbol {a} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - \gamma \| \boldsymbol {a} \| _ {2} \sqrt {\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {a ^ {*}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbb {e} \mathbb {i} (a _ {0 , t} = a ^ {*}) \phi_ {l} (x _ {0 , t + 1}) \phi_ {l} ^ {\top} (x _ {0 , t + 1}) \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {a ^ {*}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \boldsymbol {a}} = (1 - \gamma) \| \boldsymbol {a} \| _ {2} ^ {2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

under (c3). consequently, we have

$$
\| \omega_ {a ^ {*}} ^ {1 / 2} [ \mathbb {e} \phi_ {l} (x _ {0, t}) \mathbb {i} (a _ {0, t} = a ^ {*}) \{\phi_ {l} (x _ {0, t}) - \gamma \phi_ {l} (x _ {0, t + 1}) \} ] ^ {- 1} \omega_ {a ^ {*}} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {2} \leq (1 - \gamma).
$$

as such, the left-hand-side of (c.28) is upper bounded by

$$
\begin{array}{l} (1 - \gamma) ^ {- 2} \left\{\mathbb {e} \int_ {\mathbb {x}} \phi_ {l} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {a ^ {*}} ^ {- 1} \left\{\int_ {\mathbb {x}} \phi_ {l} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\} = (1 - \gamma) ^ {- 2} p _ {a ^ {*}} ^ {- 1} \left\{\int_ {\mathbb {x}} \phi_ {l} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\} ^ {\top} \\ \times \left\{\mathbb {e} \phi_ {0, l} (x _ {0, t}) \phi_ {0, l} ^ {\top} (x _ {0, t}) \right\} ^ {- 1} \left\{\int_ {\mathbb {x}} \phi_ {l} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\} = (1 - \gamma) ^ {- 2} p _ {a ^ {*}} ^ {- 1} h (\phi_ {0, l}). \\ \end{array}
$$

this completes the proof.

# c.3 additional details on on-policy evaluation

in this section, we show our proposed ci in section 4 achieves nominal converge. to simplify the analysis, we focus on the setting where  $k$  is finite,  $t(1) = \dots = t(k) = t$  and  $l(1) = \dots = l(k) = l$ . when  $k$  diverges, the sequences  $\{t(k)\}_{k\geq 1}$  and  $\{l(k)\}_{k\geq 1}$

shall be properly chosen to reduce the bias of the value estimates. we leave this for future research.

similar to appendix a.2, we assume the estimated policy  $\widehat{\pi}_{\mathcal{i}} \in \pi$  with probability 1, for any  $\mathcal{i}$ . in on-policy settings, the behavior policy  $b_{\pi}$  is a function of the estimated policy  $\pi \in \pi$ . for instance, when an  $\epsilon$ -greedy policy is used to determine the behavior policy, then we have  $b_{\pi} = (1 - \epsilon)\pi + \epsilon \pi^{*}$  where  $\pi^{*}$  denotes a uniform random policy. let  $\mathcal{b} = \{b_{\pi} : \pi \in \pi\}$ .

for any behavior policy  $b \in \mathcal{b}$ , consider a markov chain  $\{(x_{0,t,b}, a_{0,t,b})\}_{t \geq 0}$  generated by this behavior policy. let  $y_{0,t,b}$  be the realization of the immediate reward at time  $t$ . let  $\mu_b$  denote the limiting distribution of the markov chain  $\{x_{0,t,b}\}_{t \geq 0}$ , and  $\mathcal{p}_x^{t,b}(\cdot | x)$  be its  $t$ -step transition kernel. for any  $x \in \mathbb{x}, a \in \mathcal{a}$ , define  $\omega_{\pi, b}(x, a)$  as

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \left\{y _ {0, 0, b} + \gamma \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {a}} \pi (a | x _ {0, 1, b}) q (\pi ; x _ {0, 1, b}, a) - q (\pi ; x _ {0, 0, b}, a _ {0, 0, b}) \right\} ^ {2} | x _ {0, 0, b} = x, a _ {0, 0, b} = a \right].
$$

we introduce the following conditions.

(a2').) assume  $\nu_0$  and  $q$  are uniformly bounded away from 0 and  $\infty$  on their supports.  
(a3').) assume (i) and (ii) hold if  $t \to \infty$  and (iii) holds if  $t$  is bounded.

(i)  $\inf_{\pi \in \pi, b \in \mathcal{b}} \lambda_{\min} \left[ \int_{x \in \mathbb{x}} \sum_{a \in \mathcal{a}} \{\pmb{\xi}(x, a) \pmb{\xi}^\top(x, a) - \gamma^2 \pmb{u}_{\pi}(x, a) \pmb{u}_{\pi}^\top(x, a) \} b(a|x) \mu_b(x) dx \right] \geq \bar{c}$  for some constant  $\bar{c} > 0$ .  
(ii) there exists some function  $m(\cdot)$  on  $\mathbb{x}$  and some constant  $\rho < 1$  such that

$$
\sup  _ {\pi \in \pi} \int_ {x \in \mathbb {x}} m (x) \mu_ {b _ {\pi}} (x) d x <   + \infty ,
$$

and

$$
\sup  _ {\pi \in \pi} \| \mathcal {p} _ {x} ^ {t, b _ {\pi}} (\cdot | x) - \mu_ {b _ {\pi}} (\cdot) \| _ {t v} \leq m (x) \rho^ {t}, \quad \forall t \geq 0.
$$

(iii) there exists some constant  $\bar{c} > 0$  such that

$$
\inf _ {\pi \in \pi , b \in \mathcal {b}} \lambda_ {\min } [ \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \mathbb {e} \{\pmb {\xi} (x _ {0, t, b}, a _ {0, t, b}) \pmb {\xi} (x _ {0, t, b}, a _ {0, t, b}) ^ {\top} - \gamma^ {2} \pmb {u} _ {\pi} (x _ {0, t, b}, a _ {0, t, b}) \pmb {u} _ {\pi} ^ {\top} (x _ {0, t, b}, a _ {0, t, b}) \} ] \geq \bar {c} t.
$$

(a4') for any  $1 \leq k \leq k$ , we have  $\mathbb{e}|v(\widehat{\pi}_{\bar{\mathcal{i}}_k}; \mathbb{g}) - v(\pi^*; \mathbb{g})| = o(|\bar{\mathcal{i}}_k|^{-b_0})$ , for some  $b_0 > 1/2$  such that  $(nt)^{b_0 - 1/2} \gg \| \int_x \phi_l(x) \mathbb{g}(dx) \|_2^{-1}$ , where the big- $o$  term is uniform in  $\mathcal{i}$ .

theorem 6 assume  $a1$ ,  $a2' - a4'$  hold. suppose  $l = o\{\sqrt{nt} / \log(nt)\}$  and  $l^{2p/d} \gg nt\{1 + \|\int_{x} \phi_{l}(x) \mathbb{g}(dx)\|_{2}^{-2}\}$ . assume there exists some constant  $c_{0} \geq 1$  such that  $\omega_{\pi,b}(x, a) \geq c_{0}^{-1}$  for any  $x, a, \pi, b$  and  $pr(\max_{0 \leq t \leq t-1} |y_{0,t}| \leq c_{0}) = 1$ . then as either  $n \to \infty$  or  $t \to \infty$ ,

$$
\sqrt {n t (k - 1)} \widetilde {\sigma} ^ {- 1} (\mathbb {g}) \{\widetilde {v} (\mathbb {g}) - v (\widehat {\pi}; \mathbb {g}) \} \stackrel {d} {\to} n (0, 1),
$$

$$
\sqrt {n t (k - 1)} \widetilde {\sigma} ^ {- 1} (\mathbb {g}) \{\widetilde {v} (\mathbb {g}) - v (\pi^ {*}; \mathbb {g}) \} \xrightarrow {d} n (0, 1).
$$

proof of theorem 6 is omitted for brevity.

# d additional numerical results

# d.1 data-adaptive selection of  $l$

we apply the proposed method detailed in section 7.2 to scenario (b) where the treatment assignment mechanism depends on the observed state, to investigate the finite sample performance of the resulting ci. specifically, we apply the random forest algorithm to learn the conditional mean function  $\mu$  and the behavior policy  $b$ . we assume  $\sigma(a,x)$  is a constant function of  $(a,x)$  and estimated it by

$$
\widehat {\sigma} = \frac {1}{n t} \sum_ {i, t} \{x _ {i, t + 1} - \widehat {\mu} (a _ {i, t}, x _ {i, t}) \} \{x _ {i, t + 1} - \widehat {\mu} (a _ {i, t}, x _ {i, t}) \} ^ {\top}.
$$

we use the tensor product b-spline basis for  $\phi_l$ , as in section 5. note that the state is a two-dimensional vector,  $l$  is selected among the set  $\{4^2, 5^2, 6^2, 7^2, 8^2, 9^2\}$ . specifically, we choose  $l$  such that the resulting ci is the shortest among all cis whose coverage probabilities are above  $93\%$ . if no such ci exists, we select the ci with the highest coverage probability.

we report the ecp and al of the resulting ci in the left and middle panels of figure 7. it can be seen that ecp is close to the nominal level in all cases and al decays as either  $n$  or  $t$  increases. in the right panel of figure 7, we report the number of basis functions that is being selected most by the proposed method as a function of  $n$  and  $t$  (denote by  $l(n,t)$ ). it is clear from figure 7 that  $l(n,t)$  increases with the total number of observations  $nt$ . this is consistent with the following intuition: as  $nt$  increases, more basis functions are needed to reduce the approximation error and guarantee the nominal coverage of the resulting ci.

# d.2 sensitivity analysis

# d.2.1 sensitivity test for  $\eta$

in this section, we conduct the sensitivity test for the parameter  $\eta$  in the number of basis  $l = \lfloor (nt)^{\eta}\rfloor$ . we consider the simulation of the off-policy evaluation with a fixed target policy in section 5.1. for scenario (a), (b) and (c), we set  $n = 100$ ,  $t = 100$  and the different  $\eta$ 's are chosen from  $(0.25, 0.30, 0.35, 0.40, 3/7, 0.45)$ . the result of the ecps are plotted in figure 8 where all the ecps are close to the nominal coverage rate 0.95. it shows that the results of the coverage are not sensitive to the different choices of  $\eta$ .

# d.2.2 sensitivity test for  $\gamma$

in figure 9, we report the ecp and al of the proposed ci and the mse of our value estimate under scenario b where the target policy is fixed, with  $\gamma = 0.3$  and 0.7. in figure 10, we report the ecp and al of the proposed ci and the mse of our value estimate under scenario b where the target policy is an estimated optimal policy, with  $\gamma = 0.3$  and 0.7. it can be seen that findings are very similar to those with  $\gamma = 0.5$ .

in tables 4 and 5, we report the ecp, al and mse of the proposed method and drl under scenario (c) where the target policy is fixed, with  $\gamma = 0.3$  and 0.7. it can be seen that the proposed ci achieves nominal coverage in all cases. when  $\gamma = 0.3$ , ecp of the drl method is well below the nominal level in all cases. when  $\gamma = 0.7$ , the al and mse of the proposed ci are much smaller than those based on drl.

# d.4 additional real data results

we use our real data example to discuss the issue of over-fitting in this section. specifically, we apply the proposed method in section 3.2.2 to evaluate the optimal value  $v(\pi^{opt};x_{i,0})$  starting from the initial state variable  $x_{i,0}$ , for  $i = 1,2,\dots ,6$ . when the initial starting time is 8:00 am in day 1, cis for patient 5 and patient 6 are [−6.288, 3.287] and [−6.313, 10.644], respectively. both upper bounds are positive. however, according to our definition, the immediate reward is nonpositive. as such, the value and q-function shall be nonpositive as well. this reflects one of the drawback of the proposed method. the resulting q-estimator might suffer from over-fitting, leading to an unbounded outcome.

specifically, it is due to that the matrix  $\widehat{\sigma}_{\pi}$  is close to singular. note that the regression coefficients  $\widehat{\beta}_{\pi}$  are computed by solving the linear equation

$$
\widehat {\sigma} _ {\pi} \widehat {\beta} _ {\pi} = \frac {1}{\sum_ {i} t _ {i}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t _ {i} - 1} \xi_ {i, t} y _ {i, t}.
$$

in our data example, the number of basis function equals 12. as such,  $\widehat{\sigma}_{\pi}$  is a 12 by 12

![](images/c0bc8256d44eb42a8ffdfa79f0cbdb85b1a93279ade45b3dbf333f84f17d72e0.jpg)

![](images/63d41a54773dd4e60529f2cfe935ebb157deb77dae293b5a02a3d9443dbe17f3.jpg)  
figure 13: empirical coverage probabilities and average lengths of cis constructed by the proposed method under scenarios (a), with different choices of  $n$  and  $t$ . the target policy is an estimated optimal policy. the reference distribution  $\mathbb{g}$  corresponds to  $(0.5, 0.5)^{\top}$  and  $(-0.5, -0.5)^{\top}$ , from top plots to bottom plots.

![](images/0a80ef5aba006d9761429804c3921410512cb3fcf5b24ac6079259232fbc1ea9.jpg)

![](images/29ffd52f777b2bc1b2dc1e028a040987814629fc8ece67328668740ec5727105.jpg)

matrix. when it is close to singular, the resulting q-estimator might be unbounded.

to avoid offer-fitting, we note that in theory,  $\widehat{\sigma}_{\pi}$  is a positive definite matrix under condition (a3)(i). this motivates us to compute  $\widehat{\beta}_{\pi}$  by solving

$$
(\widehat {\boldsymbol {\sigma}} _ {\pi} + \lambda i) \widehat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {\pi} = \frac {1}{\sum_ {i} t _ {i}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t _ {i} - 1} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {i, t} y _ {i, t},
$$

where  $i$  denotes the identity matrix. as long as  $\lambda$  satisfies  $\lambda = o(n^{-1}t^{-1})$ , the proposed ci remains valid. in our real data example, we set  $\lambda = 1\times 10^{-9}$ . the resulting cis for patient 5 and patient 6 are  $[-8.034, - 5.667]$  and  $[-9.866, - 7.104]$ . both upper bounds are strictly negative.

# e technical proofs

for any two positive sequences  $\{a_t\}_{t \geq 1}$  and  $\{b_t\}_{t \geq 1}$ , we write  $a_t \preceq b_t$  if there exists some constant  $c > 0$  such that  $a_t \leq c b_t$  for any  $t$ . the notation  $a_t \preceq 1$  means  $a_t = o(1)$ . we will use  $c, \bar{c} > 0$  to denote some universal constants whose values are allowed to change

from place to place. let  $q_{x}(\cdot |x)$  denote the density function of  $\mathcal{p}_x(\cdot |x)$ . define  $\mathbb{s}^{ml - 1}$  as the unit sphere  $\{\pmb {v}\in \mathbb{r}^{ml}:||\pmb {v}||_2 = 1\}$ . when splines are used to estimate the q-function, we assume the internal knots are equally spaced.

the rest of the section is organized as follows. we first present the proof sketches for theorems 1-4. we next present the detailed technical proofs.

# e.1 a sketch for the proof of theorem 1

we provide an outline for the proof in this section. the detailed proof can be found in section e.5 of the supplementary article. we break the proof into three steps. in the first step, we show the estimator  $\widehat{\beta}_{\pi}$  satisfies

$$
\widehat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} = \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {\pi} ^ {- 1} \left(\frac {1}{n t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {i, t} \varepsilon_ {i, t}\right) + o _ {p} \left(l ^ {- p / d}\right) + o _ {p} \left\{l (n t) ^ {- 1} \log (n t) \right\}, \tag {e.29}
$$

where  $\pmb{\sigma}_{\pi} = \mathbb{e}\widehat{\pmb{\sigma}}_{\pi}$ . the proof of (e.29) relies on some random matrix inequalities established in lemma 3 of the supplementary article.

in the second step, we show the linear representation in (3.12) holds. the proof of (3.12) relies on the convergence rate of  $\widehat{\beta}$  established in the first step and some additional random matrix inequalities in lemma 4.

in the last step, we show the leading term on the rhs of (3.12) is asymptotically normal, based on the martingale central limit theorem. the completes the proof of theorem 1.

# e.2 a sketch for the proof of theorem 2

similar to (3.15), for each  $1 \leq k \leq k$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\widehat {v} _ {\mathcal {i} _ {k + 1}} \left(\widehat {\pi} _ {\bar {\mathcal {i}} _ {k}} ; \mathbb {g}\right) - v \left(\widehat {\pi} _ {\bar {\mathcal {i}} _ {k}} ; \mathbb {g}\right)}{(n t / k) ^ {- 1 / 2} \widehat {\sigma} _ {\mathcal {i} _ {k + 1}} \left(\widehat {\pi} _ {\bar {\mathcal {i}} _ {k}} ; \mathbb {g}\right)} \tag {e.30} \\ { = } { \frac { ( n t / k ) ^ { - \frac { 1 } { 2 } } } { \sigma ( \widehat { \pi } _ { \bar { \mathcal { i } } _ { k } } ; \mathbb { g } ) } \sum _ { ( i , t ) \in \mathcal { i } _ { k + 1 } } \left\{ \int _ { x \in \mathbb { x } } \boldsymbol { u } _ { \widehat { \pi } _ { \bar { \mathcal { i } } _ { k } } } ( x ) \mathbb { g } ( d x ) \right\} ^ { \top } \boldsymbol { \sigma } _ { \widehat { \pi } _ { \bar { \mathcal { i } } _ { k } } } ^ { - 1 } \boldsymbol { \xi } _ { i , t } \varepsilon _ { i , t } ( \widehat { \pi } _ { \bar { \mathcal { i } } _ { k } } ) + r _ { k } ^ { ( 1 ) } , \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $r_{k}^{(1)}$  denotes the remainder term and

$$
\varepsilon_ {i, t} (\widehat {\pi} _ {\bar {\mathcal {i}} _ {k}}) = y _ {i, t} + \gamma \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {a}} q (\widehat {\pi} _ {\bar {\mathcal {i}} _ {k}}; x _ {i, t + 1}, a) \widehat {\pi} _ {\bar {\mathcal {i}} _ {k}} (a | x _ {i, t + 1}) - q (\widehat {\pi} _ {\bar {\mathcal {i}} _ {k}}; x _ {i, t}, a _ {i, t}).
$$

since (3.16) is satisfied, we have  $\mathbb{e}\{\varepsilon_{i,t}(\widehat{\pi}_{\bar{\mathcal{i}}_k})|o_k\} = 0$ . conditional on the data in  $o_{k}$ ,  $\widehat{\pi}_{\bar{\mathcal{i}}_k}$  is a deterministic rule. the rhs of (e.30) is thus equivalent to a mean-zero martingale.

when (a5) is satisfied, we have

$$
\frac {\widehat {v} _ {\mathcal {i} _ {k + 1}} \left(\widehat {\pi} _ {\overline {{\mathcal {i}}} _ {k}} ; \mathbb {g}\right) - v \left(\widehat {\pi} _ {\overline {{\mathcal {i}}} _ {k}} ; \mathbb {g}\right)}{(n t / k) ^ {- 1 / 2} \widehat {\sigma} _ {\mathcal {i} _ {k + 1}} \left(\widehat {\pi} _ {\overline {{\mathcal {i}}} _ {k}} ; \mathbb {g}\right)} = \frac {\widehat {v} _ {\mathcal {i} _ {k + 1}} \left(\widehat {\pi} _ {\overline {{\mathcal {i}}} _ {k}} ; \mathbb {g}\right) - v \left(\pi^ {*} ; \mathbb {g}\right)}{(n t / k) ^ {- 1 / 2} \widehat {\sigma} _ {\mathcal {i} _ {k + 1}} \left(\widehat {\pi} _ {\overline {{\mathcal {i}}} _ {k}} ; \mathbb {g}\right)} + r _ {k} ^ {(2)}, \tag {e.31}
$$

for some remainder term  $r_{k}^{(2)}$ . suppose  $r_{k}^{(1)}$  and  $r_{k}^{(2)}$  satisfy certain convergence rates. combining (e.31) together with (e.30) yields,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\widetilde {v} (\mathbb {g}) - v \left(\pi^ {*} ; \mathbb {g}\right)}{\left\{n t (k - 1) / k \right\} ^ {- 1 / 2} \widetilde {\sigma} (\mathbb {g})} \tag {e.32} \\ { = } { \sqrt { \frac { n t ( k - 1 ) } { k } } \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { k - 1 } \sum _ { ( i , t ) \in \mathcal { i } _ { k + 1 } } \frac { 1 } { \sigma ( \widehat { \pi } _ { \bar { \mathcal { i } } _ { k } } ; \mathbb { g } ) } \left\{ \int _ { x \in \mathbb { x } } \boldsymbol { u } _ { \widehat { \pi } _ { \bar { \mathcal { i } } _ { k } } } ( x ) \mathbb { g } ( d x ) \right\} ^ { \top } \boldsymbol { \sigma } _ { \widehat { \pi } _ { \bar { \mathcal { i } } _ { k } } } ^ { - 1 } \boldsymbol { \xi } _ { i , t } \varepsilon _ { i , t } ( \widehat { \pi } _ { \bar { \mathcal { i } } _ { k } } ) + o _ { p } ( 1 ) , } \\ \end{array}
$$

due to our use of the inverse weighting trick. theorem 2 thus follows from the martingale central limit theorem.

# e.3 a sketch for the proofs of theorems 3 and 4

proofs of theorems 3 and 4 are divided into two steps. in the first step, we decompose the value difference  $v(\pi^{opt}; \mathbb{g}) - v(\widehat{\pi}_{\mathcal{i}}; \mathbb{g})$  into the sum of an infinite series and provide upper bounds for all the terms in the series. in the second step, we use the margin-type condition a5 to further characterize these upper bounds. we only present the first step in this section.

for  $j = 0,1,2,\ldots$ , define a time-dependent policy  $\widehat{\pi}_{\mathcal{i}}^{(j)}$  that executes  $\widehat{\pi}_{\mathcal{i}}$  at the first  $j$  time points and then follows  $\pi^{opt}$ . by definition, we have  $\pi^{opt} = \widehat{\pi}_{\mathcal{i}}^{(0)}$  and  $\widehat{\pi}_{\mathcal{i}} = \widehat{\pi}_{\mathcal{i}}^{(\infty)}$ . notice that

$$
v \left(\pi^ {o p t}; \mathbb {g}\right) - v \left(\widehat {\pi} _ {\mathcal {i}} ^ {(1)}; \mathbb {g}\right) = \int_ {x} \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {a}} q ^ {o p t} (x, a) \left\{\pi^ {o p t} (a | x) - \widehat {\pi} _ {\mathcal {i}} (a | x) \right\} \mathbb {g} (d x). \tag {e.33}
$$

moreover, for any  $j\geq 1$

$$
\begin{array}{l} v (\widehat {\pi} _ {\mathcal {i}} ^ {(j)}; \mathbb {g}) - v (\widehat {\pi} _ {\mathcal {i}} ^ {(j + 1)}; \mathbb {g}) = \int_ {x} \sum_ {t \geq 0} \gamma^ {t} \{\mathbb {e} ^ {\widehat {\pi} _ {\mathcal {i}} ^ {(j)}} (y _ {0, t} | x _ {0, 0} = x) - \mathbb {e} ^ {\widehat {\pi} _ {\mathcal {i}} ^ {(j + 1)}} (y _ {0, t} | x _ {0, 0} = x) \} \mathbb {g} (d x) \\ = \int_ {x} \sum_ {t \geq j} \gamma^ {t} \left\{\mathbb {e} ^ {\widehat {\pi} _ {\mathcal {i}} ^ {(j)}} \left(y _ {0, t} | x _ {0, 0} = x\right) - \mathbb {e} ^ {\widehat {\pi} _ {\mathcal {i}} ^ {(j + 1)}} \left(y _ {0, t} | x _ {0, 0} = x\right) \right\} \mathbb {g} (d x). \\ \end{array}
$$

let  $q_{x}^{(j)}(\cdot |x)$  be the density function of  $x_{0,j}$  conditional on  $x_{0,0} = x$ , following the estimated policy  $\widehat{\pi}_{\mathcal{i}}$  at the first  $j$  time points, we have

$$
\sum_ {t \geq j} \gamma^ {t} \mathbb {e} ^ {\widehat {\pi} _ {\mathcal {i}} ^ {(j)}} (y _ {0, t} | x _ {0, j}) = \gamma^ {j} \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {a}} q ^ {o p t} (x _ {0, j}, a) \pi^ {o p t} (a | x _ {0, j}),
$$

$$
\sum_ {t \geq j} \gamma^ {t} \mathbb {e} ^ {\widehat {\pi} _ {\mathcal {i}} ^ {(j + 1)}} (y _ {0, t} | x _ {0, j}) = \gamma^ {j} \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {a}} q ^ {o p t} (x _ {0, j}, a) \widehat {\pi} _ {\mathcal {i}} (a | x _ {0, j}).
$$

it follows that

$$
v (\widehat {\pi} _ {\mathcal {i}} ^ {(j)}; \mathbb {g}) - v (\widehat {\pi} _ {\mathcal {i}} ^ {(j + 1)}; \mathbb {g}) = \gamma^ {j} \int_ {x, x ^ {\prime} \in \mathbb {x}} \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {a}} q ^ {o p t} (x ^ {\prime}, a) \{\pi^ {o p t} (a | x ^ {\prime}) - \widehat {\pi} _ {\mathcal {i}} (a | x ^ {\prime}) \} q _ {x} ^ {(j)} (x ^ {\prime} | x) d x ^ {\prime} \mathbb {g} (d x).
$$

by a1, we have  $\sup_{x,x',a}q(x'|x,a)\leq c$ . under the markov assumption,

$$
q _ {x} ^ {(j)} (x ^ {\prime} | x) = \int_ {y} \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {a}} q (x ^ {\prime} | y, a) \widehat {\pi} _ {\mathcal {i}} (a | y) q _ {x} ^ {(j - 1)} (y | x) d y \le c \int_ {y} q _ {x} ^ {(j - 1)} (y | x) d y = c.
$$

in addition,  $\sum_{a\in \mathcal{a}}q^{opt}(x',a)\{\pi^{opt}(a|x') - \widehat{\pi}_{\mathcal{i}}(a|x')\} \geq 0$  for any  $x^{\prime}$ , by the definition of  $\pi^{opt}$ . therefore, we obtain

$$
v \left(\widehat {\pi} _ {\mathcal {i}} ^ {(j)}; \mathbb {g}\right) - v \left(\widehat {\pi} _ {\mathcal {i}} ^ {(j + 1)}; \mathbb {g}\right) \leq c \gamma^ {j} \int_ {x} \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {a}} q ^ {o p t} (x, a) \left\{\pi^ {o p t} (a | x) - \widehat {\pi} _ {\mathcal {i}} (a | x) \right\} d x. \tag {e.34}
$$

by a1, the reward function  $r(\cdot ,\cdot)$  is uniformly bounded. this further implies that  $q^{opt}(\cdot ,\cdot)$  is uniformly bounded. therefore,  $\sum_{j\geq t}v(\widehat{\pi}_{\mathcal{i}}^{(j)};\mathbb{g}) - v(\widehat{\pi}_{\mathcal{i}}^{(j + 1)};\mathbb{g})\to 0$  as  $t\to \infty$ . it follows from (e.33) and (e.34) that

$$
\begin{array}{l} v (\pi^ {o p t}; \mathbb {g}) - v (\widehat {\pi} _ {\mathcal {i}}; \mathbb {g}) = \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {+ \infty} \{v (\widehat {\pi} _ {\mathcal {i}} ^ {(j)}; \mathbb {g}) - v (\widehat {\pi} _ {\mathcal {i}} ^ {(j + 1)}; \mathbb {g}) \} \\ \leq \int_ {x} \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {a}} q ^ {\text {o p t}} (x, a) \left\{\pi^ {\text {o p t}} (a | x) - \widehat {\pi} _ {\mathcal {i}} (a | x) \right\} \mathbb {g} (d x) (e.35) \\ + \frac {c \gamma}{1 - \gamma} \int_ {x} \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {a}} q ^ {\text {o p t}} (x, a) \left\{\pi^ {\text {o p t}} (a | x) - \widehat {\pi} _ {\mathcal {i}} (a | x) \right\} d x. (e.36) \\ \end{array}
$$

let  $\lambda$  denote the lebesgue measure on  $\mathbb{r}^d$ . in sections e.11 and e.12, we use a5 to further bound (e.35) and (e.36).

# e.4 proof of lemma 1

since  $\mathbb{x}$  is compact, condition (a1) implies that  $\sup_{x\in \mathbb{x},a\in \mathcal{a}}|r(x,a)|\leq r$  for some  $0 < r < +\infty$ . under cmia, we have

$$
q (\pi ; x, a) = \sum_ {t \geq 0} \gamma^ {t} \mathbb {e} ^ {\pi} (y _ {0, t}, x _ {0, 0} = x, a _ {0, 0} = a) = \sum_ {t \geq 0} \gamma^ {t} \mathbb {e} ^ {\pi} \{r (x _ {0, t}, a _ {0, t}) | x _ {0, 0} = x, a _ {0, 0} = a \}.
$$

as a result, we obtain

$$
\sup  _ {\pi , x, a} | q (\pi ; x, a) | \leq \frac {r}{1 - \gamma}. \tag {e.37}
$$

by the bellman equation, we obtain

$$
q (\pi ; x, a) = r (x, a) + \gamma \int_ {x ^ {\prime}} \sum_ {a ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {a}} q (\pi ; x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}) \pi (a ^ {\prime} | x ^ {\prime}) q (x ^ {\prime} | x, a) d x ^ {\prime}.
$$

since  $r(\cdot ,a)$  is  $p$  -smooth for any  $a\in \mathcal{a}$  , it suffices to show

$$
t (\pi ; x, a) = \int_ {x ^ {\prime}} \sum_ {a ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {a}} q (\pi ; x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}) \pi (a ^ {\prime} | x ^ {\prime}) q (x ^ {\prime} | x, a) d x ^ {\prime},
$$

is  $p$ -smooth for any  $a \in \mathcal{a}$  and any policy  $\pi$ .

for any function  $h(\cdot)$  defined on  $\mathbb{x}$ , let  $\partial_j h(x)$  denote the partial derivative  $\partial h(x) / \partial x_j$ . without loss of generality, suppose  $p > 1$  such that  $\partial_j p(x'|x,a)$  exists for any  $j$ . in the following, we show  $\partial_j t(\pi; x,a)$  exists for any  $j$ . let

$$
e _ {j} = (\underbrace {0 , \ldots , 0} _ {j - 1}, 1, \underbrace {0 , \ldots , 0} _ {d - j}) ^ {\top}, \forall j = 1, \ldots , d.
$$

for any  $\delta \in \mathbb{r}$ , consider the limit

$$
\mathrm {r e} (j, \delta) = \frac {t (\pi ; x + e _ {j} \delta , a) - t (\pi ; x , a)}{\delta} - \int_ {x ^ {\prime}} \sum_ {a ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {a}} q (\pi ; x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}) \pi (a ^ {\prime} | x ^ {\prime}) \partial_ {j} q (x ^ {\prime} | x, a) d x ^ {\prime},
$$

as  $j\to \infty$  . by the mean value theorem, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} | \mathrm {r e} (j, \delta) | \leq \int_ {x ^ {\prime}} \sum_ {a ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {a}} | q (\pi ; x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}) | \pi (a ^ {\prime} | x ^ {\prime}) \left| \frac {q (x ^ {\prime} | x + e _ {j} \delta , a) - q (x ^ {\prime} | x , a)}{\delta} - \partial_ {j} q (x ^ {\prime} | x, a) \right| d x ^ {\prime} \\ \leq \int_ {x ^ {\prime}} \sum_ {a ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {a}} | q (\pi ; x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}) | \pi (a ^ {\prime} | x ^ {\prime}) | \partial_ {j} q (x ^ {\prime} | x + e _ {j} \theta_ {x} \delta , a) - \partial_ {j} q (x ^ {\prime} | x, a) | d x ^ {\prime}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $0 \leq \theta_{x} \leq 1$  for all  $x$ . when  $1 < p \leq 2$ , we have  $\lfloor p \rfloor = 1$ . it follows from condition a1 that

$$
| \partial_ {j} q (x ^ {\prime} | x + e _ {j} \theta_ {x} \delta , a) - \partial_ {j} q (x ^ {\prime} | x, a) | \leq c \delta^ {p - 1}.
$$

when  $p > 2$ ,  $|\partial_j\partial_jq(x'|x,a)|$  exists and is bounded by  $c$  for any  $x^{\prime}$ ,  $x$  and  $a$ . it follows from the mean value theorem that

$$
\left| \partial_ {j} q \left(x ^ {\prime} \mid x + e _ {j} \theta_ {x} \delta , a\right) - \partial_ {j} q \left(x ^ {\prime} \mid x, a\right) \right| \leq c \delta .
$$

in either case, we have that

$$
| \mathrm {r e} (j, \delta) | \leq c \max  (\delta , \delta^ {p - 1}) \int_ {x ^ {\prime}} \sum_ {a ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {a}} | q (\pi ; x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}) | \pi (a ^ {\prime} | x ^ {\prime}) d x ^ {\prime}.
$$

by (e.37) and that  $\mathbb{x}$  is compact, we obtain  $\operatorname{re}(j,\delta)\to 0$  as  $\delta \rightarrow 0$ . this implies that  $\partial_j t(\pi ;x,a)$  exists for any  $x\in \mathbb{x}$ ,  $a\in \mathcal{a}$  and equals

$$
\int_ {x ^ {\prime}} \sum_ {a ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {a}} q (\pi ; x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}) \pi (a ^ {\prime} | x ^ {\prime}) \partial_ {j} q (x ^ {\prime} | x, a) d x ^ {\prime}.
$$

in addition, it follows from a1 and (e.37) that

$$
| \partial_ {j} t (\pi ; x, a) | \leq \frac {r c}{1 - \gamma} \lambda (\mathbb {x}), \quad \forall j, x, a, \pi ,
$$

where  $\lambda(\cdot)$  denotes the lebesgue measure. using the same arguments, we can show for any  $d$ -tuple  $\alpha = (\alpha_{1}, \ldots, \alpha_{d})^{\top}$  of nonnegative integers that satisfies  $\| \alpha \|_{1} \leq \lfloor p \rfloor$ ,

$$
d ^ {\alpha} t (\pi ; \cdot , a) = \int_ {x ^ {\prime}} \sum_ {a ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {a}} q \left(\pi ; x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}\right) \pi \left(a ^ {\prime} \mid x ^ {\prime}\right) d ^ {\alpha} q \left(x ^ {\prime} \mid , a\right) d x ^ {\prime}, \tag {e.38}
$$

and

$$
\sup  _ {\pi} \sup  _ {x \in \mathbb {x}, a \in \mathcal {a}} | d ^ {\alpha} t (\pi ; x, a) | \leq \frac {r c}{1 - \gamma} \lambda (\mathbb {x}). \tag {e.39}
$$

moreover, by a1, (e.37) and (e.38), we have for any  $d$ -tuple  $\alpha$  with  $\| \alpha \|_1 = \lfloor p \rfloor$  that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| d ^ {\alpha} t (\pi ; x, a) - d ^ {\alpha} t (\pi ; y, a) \right\| _ {2} \\ \leq \int_ {x ^ {\prime}} \sum_ {a ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {a}} | q (\pi ; x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}) | \pi (a ^ {\prime} | x ^ {\prime}) | d ^ {\alpha} q (x ^ {\prime} | x, a) - d ^ {\alpha} q (x ^ {\prime} | y, a) | d x ^ {\prime} \leq \frac {r c}{1 - \gamma} \lambda (\mathbb {x}) \| x - y \| _ {2} ^ {p - \lfloor p \rfloor}. \\ \end{array}
$$

this together with (e.38) implies that  $t(\pi; \cdot, a) \in \lambda(p, rc\lambda(\mathbb{x})(1 - \gamma)^{-1})$  for any  $\pi$  and  $a$ . the proof is thus completed.

# e.5 proof of theorem 1

we introduce the following lemmas before proving theorem 1. in the proof of theorem 1 and lemma 2-5, we will omit the subscript  $\pi$  in  $\pmb{u}_{\pi}(\cdot)$ ,  $\pmb{u}_{\pi}$ ,  $\pmb{\sigma}_{\pi}$ ,  $\widehat{\pmb{\sigma}}_{\pi}$ ,  $\widehat{\pmb{\beta}}_{\pi}$ ,  $\pmb{\beta}_{\pi}^{*}$ ,  $\omega_{\pi}$ , etc, for brevity.

lemma 2 there exists some constant  $c^* \geq 1$  such that

$$
(c ^ {*}) ^ {- 1} \leq \lambda_ {\min } \left\{\int_ {x \in \mathbb {x}} \phi_ {l} (x) \phi_ {l} ^ {\top} (x) d x \right\} \leq \lambda_ {\max } \left\{\int_ {x \in \mathbb {x}} \phi_ {l} (x) \phi_ {l} ^ {\top} (x) d x \right\} \leq c ^ {*}, \quad (\mathrm {e}. 4 0)
$$

and  $\sup_{x\in \mathbb{x}}\| \phi_l(x)\| _2\leq c^*\sqrt{l}$

lemma 3 suppose the conditions in theorem 1 hold. we have as either  $n \to \infty$  or  $t \to \infty$  that  $\| \sigma^{-1} \|_2 \leq 3\bar{c}^{-1}$ ,  $\| \sigma \|_2 = o(1)$ ,  $\| \widehat{\sigma} - \sigma \|_2 = o_p\{l^{1/2}(nt)^{-1/2}\log(nt)\}$ ,  $\| \widehat{\sigma}^{-1} - \sigma^{-1} \|_2 = o_p\{l^{1/2}(nt)^{-1/2}\log(nt)\}$  and  $\| \widehat{\sigma}^{-1} \| \leq 6\bar{c}^{-1}$  wpa1.

lemma 4 suppose the conditions in theorem 1 hold. we have as either  $n \to \infty$  or  $t \to \infty$  that  $\lambda_{\max}(t^{-1} \sum_{t=0}^{t-1} \mathbb{e} \pmb{\xi}_{0,t} \pmb{\xi}_{0,t}^{\top}) = o(1)$ ,  $\lambda_{\max}\{(nt)^{-1} \sum_{i=1}^{n} \sum_{t=0}^{t-1} \pmb{\xi}_{i,t} \pmb{\xi}_{i,t}^{\top}\} = o_p(1)$ ,  $\lambda_{\min}(t^{-1} \sum_{t=0}^{t-1} \mathbb{e} \pmb{\xi}_{0,t} \pmb{\xi}_{0,t}^{\top}) \geq \bar{c}/2$  and  $\lambda_{\min}\{(nt)^{-1} \sum_{i=1}^{n} \sum_{t=0}^{t-1} \pmb{\xi}_{i,t} \pmb{\xi}_{i,t}^{\top}\} \geq \bar{c}/3$  wpa1.

lemma 5  $\| \int_{x}\pmb {u}(x)\mathbb{g}(dx)\|_{2}\geq m^{-1 / 2}\| \int_{x}\phi_{l}(x)\mathbb{g}(dx)\|_{2}.$

step 1. since  $l^{2p / d} \gg nt\{1 + \left\| \int_{x}\phi_{l}(x)\mathbb{g}(dx)\right\|_{2}^{-2}\}$ , it follows from lemma 5 that  $l^{2p / d} \gg nt\{1 + \left\| \int_{x}\boldsymbol {u}(x)\mathbb{g}(dx)\right\|_{2}^{-2}\}$ . by lemma 1, there exist a set of vector  $\{\beta_a^*\}$  that satisfy (see section 2.2 of huang, 1998, for details)

$$
\sup  _ {x \in \mathbb {x}, a \in \mathcal {a}} | q (\pi ; x, a) - \phi_ {l} ^ {\top} (x) \beta_ {a} ^ {*} | \leq c l ^ {- p / d}, \tag {e.41}
$$

for some constant  $c > 0$ . let  $\beta^{*} = (\beta_{1}^{*t},\dots ,\beta_{m}^{*t})^{\top}$ , and

$$
r _ {i, t} = \gamma \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {a}} \{\phi_ {l} ^ {\top} (x _ {i, t + 1}) \beta_ {a} ^ {*} - q (\pi ; x _ {i, t + 1}, a) \} \pi (a | x _ {i, t + 1}) - \{\phi_ {l} ^ {\top} (x _ {i, t}) \beta_ {a _ {i, t}} ^ {*} - q (\pi ; x _ {i, t}, a _ {i, t}) \}.
$$

the condition  $\operatorname*{pr}(\max_{0\leq t\leq t - 1}|y_{i,t}|\leq c_0) = 1$  implies that  $|y_{i,t}|\leq c_0,\forall i,t$ , almost surely. by lemma 1 and the definition of  $p$ -smooth functions, we obtain that  $|q(\pi ;x,a)|\leq c^{\prime}$  for any  $\pi ,x,a$ . it follows that

$$
\max  _ {0 \leq t \leq t - 1, 0 \leq i \leq n} | \varepsilon_ {i, t} | \leq c _ {0} + (\gamma + 1) c ^ {\prime} \leq c _ {0} + 2 c ^ {\prime}, \tag {e.42}
$$

almost surely. in addition, it follows from (e.41) that

$$
\max  _ {0 \leq t \leq t - 1, 1 \leq i \leq n} | r _ {i, t} | \leq 2 \sup  _ {x \in \mathbb {x}, a \in \mathcal {a}} | q (\pi ; x, a) - \phi_ {l} ^ {\top} (x) \beta_ {a} ^ {*} | \leq 2 c l ^ {- p / d}. \tag {e.43}
$$

by definition, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \widehat {\pmb {\beta}} - \pmb {\beta} ^ {*} = \widehat {\sigma} ^ {- 1} \left[ \frac {1}{n t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \pmb {\xi} _ {i, t} \{y _ {i, t} - (\pmb {\xi} _ {i, t} - \gamma \pmb {u} _ {i, t + 1}) ^ {\top} \pmb {\beta} ^ {*} \} \right] \\ { = } { \widehat { \pmb { \sigma } } ^ { - 1 } \left[ \frac { 1 } { n t } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } \sum _ { t = 0 } ^ { t - 1 } \pmb { \xi } _ { i , t } \left\{ y _ { i , t } - \phi _ { l } ^ { \top } ( x _ { i , t } ) \beta _ { a _ { i , t } } ^ { * } + \gamma \sum _ { a \in \mathcal { a } } \phi _ { l } ^ { \top } ( x _ { i , t + 1 } ) \beta _ { a } ^ { * } \pi ( a | x _ { i , t + 1 } ) \right\} \right] } \\ = \widehat {\boldsymbol {\sigma}} ^ {- 1} \left\{\frac {1}{n t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {i, t} (\varepsilon_ {i, t} - r _ {i, t}) \right\} = \underbrace {\boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {- 1} \left(\frac {1}{n t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {i , t} \varepsilon_ {i , t}\right)} _ {\zeta_ {1}} \\ + \underbrace {(\widehat {\boldsymbol {\sigma}} ^ {- 1} - \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {- 1}) \left(\frac {1}{n t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {i , t} \varepsilon_ {i , t}\right)} _ {\zeta_ {2}} - \underbrace {\widehat {\boldsymbol {\sigma}} ^ {- 1} \left(\frac {1}{n t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {i , t} r _ {i , t}\right)} _ {\zeta_ {3}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

in the following, we show  $\zeta_2 = o_p\{l(nt)^{-1}\log (nt)\}$  and  $\zeta_3 = o_p(l^{-p / d})$  as either  $n\to \infty$  or  $t\rightarrow \infty$

error bound for  $\|\zeta_2\|_2$ : let  $\mathcal{f}_{i,t}$  denote the sub-dataset  $\{x_{i,t}, a_{i,t}\} \cup \{(y_{i,j}, a_{i,j}, x_{i,j})\}_{1 \leq j < t}$ . by the bellman equation in (3.7), ma and cmia, we have

$$
\mathbb {e} (\varepsilon_ {i, t} | \mathcal {f} _ {i, t}) = \mathbb {e} (\varepsilon_ {i, t} | x _ {i, t}, a _ {i, t}) = 0.
$$

notice that  $\pmb{\xi}_{i,t}$  is a function of  $x_{i,t}$  and  $a_{i,t}$  only, we have for any  $0\leq t_1 < t_2\leq t - 1$  that

$$
\mathbb {e} \varepsilon_ {i, t _ {1}} \varepsilon_ {i, t _ {2}} \pmb {\xi} _ {i, t _ {1}} ^ {\top} \pmb {\xi} _ {i, t _ {2}} = \mathbb {e} \bigl \{\varepsilon_ {i, t _ {1}} \pmb {\xi} _ {i, t _ {1}} ^ {\top} \pmb {\xi} _ {i, t _ {2}} \mathbb {e} (\varepsilon_ {i, t _ {2}} | \mathcal {f} _ {i, t _ {2}}) \bigr \} = 0.
$$

by the independence assumption, we have for any  $0 \leq t_1 < t_2 \leq t - 1$  and  $1 \leq i_1 < i_2 \leq n$  that  $\mathbb{e} \varepsilon_{i_1, t_1} \varepsilon_{i_2, t_2} \pmb{\xi}_{i_1, t_1}^{\top} \pmb{\xi}_{i_2, t_2} = 0$ . it follows that

$$
\mathbb {e} \left\| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \pmb {\xi} _ {i, t} \varepsilon_ {i, t} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \mathbb {e} \varepsilon_ {i, t} ^ {2} \pmb {\xi} _ {i, t} ^ {\top} \pmb {\xi} _ {i, t} = n \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \mathbb {e} \varepsilon_ {0, t} ^ {2} \pmb {\xi} _ {0, t} ^ {\top} \pmb {\xi} _ {0, t}.
$$

by (e.42) and lemma 2, we obtain

$$
\mathbb {e} \left\| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \pmb {\xi} _ {i, t} \varepsilon_ {i, t} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq (c _ {0} + 2 c ^ {\prime}) ^ {2} n \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \mathbb {e} \pmb {\xi} _ {0, t} ^ {\top} \pmb {\xi} _ {0, t} \leq (c _ {0} + 2 c ^ {\prime}) ^ {2} n t \sup _ {x \in \mathbb {x}} \| \phi_ {l} (x) \| _ {2} ^ {2} \preceq n t l.
$$

by markov's inequality, we obtain  $(nt)^{-1}\sum_{i = 1}^{n}\sum_{t = 0}^{t - 1}\pmb{\xi}_{i,t}\varepsilon_{i,t} = o_p\{\sqrt{l / (nt)}\}$ . combining this together with lemma 3 yields that  $\zeta_2 = o_p\{\sqrt{l / nt}\log (nt)\} o_p\{\sqrt{l / (nt)}\} = o_p\{l(nt)^{-1}\log (nt)\}$ .

error bound for  $\| \zeta_3\| _2$  : for any  $\pmb {a}\in \mathbb{r}^{ml}$  , it follows from (e.43) and cauchy-schwarz inequality that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \pmb {a} ^ {\top} \left(\frac {1}{n t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \pmb {\xi} _ {i, t} r _ {i, t}\right) \right| \leq \frac {1}{n t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} | \pmb {a} ^ {\top} \pmb {\xi} _ {i, t} | | r _ {i, t} | \leq \max _ {i, t} | r _ {i, t} | \left(\frac {1}{n t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} | \pmb {a} ^ {\top} \pmb {\xi} _ {i, t} |\right) \\ \leq 2 c l ^ {- p / d} \left(\frac {1}{n t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} | \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {i, t} |\right) \leq 2 c l ^ {- p / d} \left(\frac {1}{n t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {i, t} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {i, t} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {a}\right) ^ {1 / 2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

therefore, we obtain

$$
\left\| \frac {1}{n t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \pmb {\xi} _ {i, t} r _ {i, t} \right\| _ {2} \leq 2 c l ^ {- p / d} \lambda_ {\max} ^ {1 / 2} \left(\frac {1}{n t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \pmb {\xi} _ {i, t} \pmb {\xi} _ {i, t} ^ {\top}\right).
$$

by lemma 4, we obtain

$$
\left\| \frac {1}{n t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {i, t} r _ {i, t} \right\| _ {2} = o _ {p} (l ^ {- p / d}). \tag {e.44}
$$

by lemma 3, we have  $\| \widehat{\sigma}^{-1}\| _2 = o_p(1)$ . combining this together with (e.44) yields that  $\zeta_3 = o_p(l^{-p / d})$ .

to summarize, we have shown

$$
\widehat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} = \zeta_ {1} + o _ {p} \left(l ^ {- p / d}\right) + o _ {p} \left\{l (n t) ^ {- 1} \log (n t) \right\}. \tag {e.45}
$$

this completes the first step of the proof.

step 2: using similar arguments in bounding  $\| \zeta_2\| _2$  in step 1, we can show that  $\| \zeta_1\| _2 = o_p\{l^{1 / 2}(nt)^{-1 / 2}\}$  and thus

$$
\left\| \widehat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \right\| _ {2} = o _ {p} \left(l ^ {- p / d}\right) + o _ {p} \left\{l ^ {1 / 2} (n t) ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\}, \tag {e.46}
$$

under the condition that  $l \ll \sqrt{nt} / \log (nt)$ . notice that  $\widehat{v}(\pi; \mathbb{g})$  can be presented as  $\{\int_x \pmb{u}(x) \mathbb{g}(dx)\}^\top \widehat{\pmb{\beta}}$ . as a result,

$$
\left| \widehat {v} (\pi ; \mathbb {g}) - \left\{\int_ {x} \boldsymbol {u} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\} ^ {\top} \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} + \zeta_ {1}\right) \right| \leq \left\| \int_ {x} \boldsymbol {u} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\| _ {2} \| \widehat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} - \zeta_ {1} \| _ {2}. \quad (e. 4 7)
$$

by (e.41), we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} | \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} (x) \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} - v (\pi ; x) | = \left| \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} (x) \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} - \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {a}} q (\pi ; x, a) \pi (a | x) \right| \\ \leq \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {a}} | q (\pi ; x, a) - \phi_ {l} ^ {\top} (x) \beta_ {a} ^ {*} | \pi (a | x) \leq c l ^ {- p / d}, \\ \end{array}
$$

and hence

$$
\left| \left\{\int_ {x} \boldsymbol {u} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} - \int_ {x} v (\pi ; x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right| \leq c l ^ {- p / d}.
$$

this together with (e.47) yields that

$$
\left| \widehat {v} (\pi ; \mathbb {g}) - v (\pi ; \mathbb {g}) - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} (x) \zeta_ {1} \right| \leq \left\| \int_ {x} \boldsymbol {u} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\| _ {2} \| \widehat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} - \zeta_ {1} \| _ {2} + c l ^ {- p / d}. \tag {e.48}
$$

let  $\omega = t^{-1}\sum_{t = 0}^{t - 1}\mathbb{e}\omega (x_{0,t},a_{0,t})\pmb{\xi}_{0,t}\pmb{\xi}_{0,t}^{\top}$  and

$$
\sigma^ {2} (\pi ; \mathbb {g}) = \left\{\int_ {x} \boldsymbol {u} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\omega} (\boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {\top}) ^ {- 1} \left\{\int_ {x} \boldsymbol {u} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\}.
$$

since  $\inf_{x,a}\omega (x,a)\geq c_0^{-1}$ , it follows from lemma 4 that

$$
\lambda_ {\min } (\boldsymbol {\omega}) \geq c _ {0} ^ {- 1} \lambda_ {\min } \left(\frac {1}{t} \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {t - 1} \mathbb {e} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {0, t} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {0, t} ^ {\top}\right) \geq 3 ^ {- 1} c _ {0} ^ {- 1} \bar {c}.
$$

hence, we obtain

$$
\sigma^ {2} (\pi ; \mathbb {g}) \geq \frac {\bar {c}}{3 c _ {0}} \left\{\int_ {x} \boldsymbol {u} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {- 1} \left(\boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {\top}\right) ^ {- 1} \left\{\int_ {x} \boldsymbol {u} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\}. \tag {e.49}
$$

by lemma 3, we have  $\| \pmb{\sigma}\|_{2} = o(1)$ , or equivalently,  $\lambda_{\max}(\pmb{\sigma}^{\top}\pmb{\sigma}) = o(1)$ . this implies that  $\lambda_{\min}\{\pmb{\sigma}^{-1}(\pmb{\sigma}^{\top})^{-1}\} \geq \bar{c}$  for some constant  $\bar{c} > 0$  and hence

$$
\sigma^ {2} (\pi ; \mathbb {g}) \geq (3 c _ {0}) ^ {- 1} \bar {c} \bar {c} \left\| \int_ {x} \boldsymbol {u} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}, \tag {e.50}
$$

by (e.49). combining (e.50) together with (e.48) yields that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{\sigma (\pi ; \mathbb {g})} \left| \widehat {v} (\pi ; \mathbb {g}) - v (\pi ; \mathbb {g}) - \left\{\int_ {x} \boldsymbol {u} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\} ^ {\top} \zeta_ {1} \right| \leq \frac {\sqrt {3 c _ {0}}}{\sqrt {\bar {c} \bar {c}}} \| \widehat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} - \zeta_ {1} \| _ {2} \\ + \frac {\sqrt {3 c _ {0}} c l ^ {- p / d}}{\sqrt {\bar {c} \bar {c}} \| \int_ {x} \boldsymbol {u} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \| _ {2}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

by (e.45) and that  $l \ll \sqrt{nt} / \log(nt)$ ,  $l^{2p/d} \gg nt\{1 + \left\| \int_{x} \mathbf{u}(x) \mathbb{g}(dx) \right\|_2^{-2}\}$ , we obtain

$$
\frac {\sqrt {n t} \left\{\widehat {v} (\pi ; \mathbb {g}) - v (\pi ; \mathbb {g}) \right\}}{\sigma (\pi ; \mathbb {g})} = \frac {\sqrt {n t} \left\{\int_ {x} \boldsymbol {u} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\} ^ {\top} \zeta_ {1}}{\sigma (\pi ; x)} + o _ {p} (1). \tag {e.51}
$$

this completes the second step of the proof.

step 3: in the following, we show  $\sqrt{nt}\sigma^{-1}(\pi ;\mathbb{g})\{\int_x\pmb {u}(x)\mathbb{g}(dx)\}^\top \zeta_1\stackrel {d}{\to}n(0,1)$ . for any integer  $1\leq g\leq nt$ , let  $i(g)$  and  $t(g)$  be the quotient and the remainder of  $g + t - 1$  divided by  $t$  that satisfy

$$
g = \{i (g) - 1 \} t + t (g) + 1 \text {a n d} 0 \leq t (g) <   t.
$$

let  $\mathcal{f}^{(0)} = \{x_{1,0},a_{1,0}\}$ . then we iteratively define  $\{\mathcal{f}^{(g)}\}_{1\leq g\leq nt}$  as follows:

$$
\mathcal {f} ^ {(g)} = \mathcal {f} ^ {(g - 1)} \cup \left\{y _ {i (g), t (g)}, x _ {i (g), t (g) + 1}, a _ {i (g), t (g) + 1} \right\}, \text {i f} t (g) <   t - 1,
$$

$$
\mathcal {f} ^ {(g)} = \mathcal {f} ^ {(g - 1)} \cup \{y _ {i (g), t - 1}, x _ {i (g), t}, x _ {i (g) + 1, 0}, a _ {i (g) + 1, 0} \}, \quad \text {o t h e r w i s e}.
$$

let  $\pmb{\xi}^{(g)} = \pmb{\xi}_{i(g),t(g)}$  and  $\varepsilon^{(g)} = \varepsilon_{i(g),t(g)}$ . it follows that

$$
\sqrt {n t} \frac {\left\{\int_ {x} \boldsymbol {u} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\} ^ {\top} \zeta_ {1}}{\sigma (\pi ; x)} = \sum_ {g = 1} ^ {n t} \frac {\left\{\int_ {x} \boldsymbol {u} (x) \mathbb {g} (d x) \right\} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\xi} ^ {(g)} \varepsilon^ {(g)}}{\sqrt {n t} \sigma (\pi ; x)}. \tag {e.52}
$$

by ma, cmia and the bellman equation in (3.7), we obtain that

$$
\mathbb {e} \{\varepsilon^ {(g)} | \mathcal {f} ^ {(g - 1)} \} = \mathbb {e} \{\varepsilon^ {(g)} | x _ {i (g), t (g)}, a _ {i (g), t (g)} \} = 0.
$$

hence, the rhs of (e.52) forms a martingale with respect to the filtration  $\{\sigma(\mathcal{f}^{(g)})\}_{g \geq 0}$ , where  $\sigma(\mathcal{f}^{(g)})$  stands for the  $\sigma$ -algebra generated by  $\mathcal{f}^{(g)}$ . to show the asymptotic normality, we use a martingale central limit theorem for triangular arrays (corollary 2.8 of mcleish, 1974). this requires to verify the following two conditions:

(a)  $\max_{1\leq g\leq nt}|\{\int_x\pmb {u}(x)\mathbb{g}(dx)\}^\top \pmb{\sigma}^{-1}\pmb{\xi}^{(g)}\varepsilon^{(g)}| / \{\sqrt{nt}\sigma (\pi ;x)\} \stackrel {p}{\to}0;$  
(b)  $(nt)^{-1}\sum_{g = 1}^{nt}|\{\int_x\pmb {u}(x)\mathbb{g}(dx)\}^\top \pmb {\sigma}\pmb{\xi}^{(g)}\varepsilon^{(g)}|^2 /\{\sigma^2 (\pi ;x)\} \stackrel {p}{\to}1.$

notice that

$$
